First ans:  Crude oil was first discovered in Nigeria in 1956, specifically in the Oloibiri oilfield located in the Niger Delta region. The discovery was made by Shell-BP, a consortium of Shell and British Petroleum, and it marked the end of 50 years of unsuccessful oil exploration in the country. The first oil production from the Oloibiri field began in 1958, with an initial rate of 4,928 barrels per day. However, the exact date of the first discovery is 15 January 1956, when the discovery well Oloibiri −1 was spudded on 3 August 1955 and drilled vertical to a total depth of 12008 feet. The well was tested and it flowed at the rate of about 5,000 barrels of oil per day.
Crude oil was first discovered in Nigeria in 1956, specifically at the Oloibiri Oilfield, which was discovered on Sunday, 15 January 1956. The discovery was made by Shell Darcy, and it was initially operated by Shell-BP Petroleum Development Company of Nigeria Limited. The discovery well, Oloibiri-1, was spudded on 3 August 1955 and drilled vertical to a total depth of 12,008 feet. The well was tested and it flowed at the rate of about 5,000 barrels of oil per day, and it was deemed to be a commercial discovery. This discovery marked the beginning of oil production in Nigeria, and it paved the way for further exploration and development in the country.

First ans:  H. H. Asquith served as England's prime minister during the initial stages of WW1, from August 4, 1914, until May 1915. He was then succeeded by David Lloyd George, who formed a coalition government with the Conservatives and led the country through the remainder of the war. Asquith's leadership was marked by a reluctance to take strong measures, such as conscription and censorship, which were eventually implemented by Lloyd George. Lloyd George's government was more effective in mobilizing resources and coordinating the war effort, but his personal conduct and leadership style were criticized by some historians. Overall, the period was marked by significant challenges and transformations for the British government and society.
H. H. Asquith was the Prime Minister of England when the First World War broke out in 1914. He led the country until May 1915, when he formed a coalition government with the Conservatives. David Lloyd George took over as Prime Minister in December 1916, after Asquith's government was brought down due to the failure of the Gallipoli Campaign. Lloyd George led the country for the remainder of the war, until the Armistice in November 1918. During this time, he played a crucial role in coordinating the Allied war effort and implementing measures to boost morale and productivity on the home front.

Chavez's march was a significant event in the history of the United Farm Workers movement, and it helped to raise awareness about the struggles of farm workers in California. The march was a key moment in Chavez's career as a labor leader, and it demonstrated his commitment to nonviolent direct action. As a result of the march, the National Farm Workers Association and the Agricultural Workers Organizing Committee merged and became known as the United Farm Workers Organizing Committee, which was later chartered by the AFL-CIO.
Cesar Chavez conducted a 300-mile march to Sacramento, California in March 1966 to pressure growers and the state government to answer the demands of the Mexican American and Filipino American farm workers. This march was an attempt to bring widespread public attention to the farm workers' cause and to pressure the government to take action. The march was a significant event in the Delano grape strike and marked a turning point in the movement's efforts to gain national attention and support. Chavez's march was also a key factor in the eventual merger of the National Farm Workers Association and the Agricultural Workers Organizing Committee to form the United Farm Workers Organizing Committee. Most scholars agree that the books were written by Jewish Christians, with Luke and Acts being written by the same author, but the identity of this author is disputed. The Gospel of Matthew, Mark, and Luke are attributed to Matthew, Mark, and Luke, respectively, but the historical existence of these individuals is not universally accepted. The Gospel of John is traditionally attributed to John the Apostle, but this is also a matter of debate. The authorship of the other books in the New Testament, such as the Pauline epistles and the Book of Revelation, is also disputed, with some scholars attributing them to Paul or other early Christian leaders, while others argue that they were written by later generations of Christians.
The authorship of the New Testament books is a topic of ongoing scholarly debate and research. The traditional view is that the books were written by Jewish Christians, including the apostles and other early followers of Jesus. However, most modern scholars agree that the authors of the New Testament books are anonymous, and that the names attached to them were likely added later. The Gospel of Luke and the Acts of the Apostles are traditionally attributed to Luke the Evangelist, but many scholars reject this due to the differences between Acts and the authentic Pauline letters. The Epistle to the Hebrews is also traditionally attributed to Paul, but most modern scholars agree that it was not written by him.

First ans:  Construction on the Sydney Harbour Bridge started on 28 July 1923. This date marks the beginning of the official ceremony to mark the "turning of the first sod," which was held on the spot at Milsons Point on the north shore where two workshops to assist in building the bridge were to be constructed. The construction process took eight years to complete, with the bridge officially opening on 19 March 1932. The construction period coincided with the construction of a system of underground railways in Sydney's CBD, known today as the City Circle, and the bridge was designed with this in mind.
Construction on the Sydney Harbour Bridge started on 28 July 1923, with the official ceremony to mark the "turning of the first sod" occurring on this date. However, the construction of the approaches and approach spans began before this, and by September 1926, concrete piers to support the approach spans were in place on each side of the harbour. The building of the bridge coincided with the construction of a system of underground railways in Sydney's CBD, known today as the City Circle. The bridge was designed to carry six lanes of road traffic, flanked on each side by two railway tracks and a footpath, and construction progressed with the construction of approaches, piers, and the steelwork.

The civil war between Huáscar and Atahualpa weakened the empire, and the arrival of the Spanish conquistadors, led by Francisco Pizarro, further weakened the empire's defenses. The empire's lack of immunity to European diseases, such as smallpox, influenza, and typhus, also significantly contributed to its downfall. The Inca Empire's inability to adapt to these new diseases, combined with its internal conflicts and external invasions, ultimately led to its collapse.

First ans:  The movement of food in the food pipe is called peristalsis, which is a radially symmetrical contraction and relaxation of muscles that propagates in a wave down a tube, in an anterograde direction. This process is essential for the proper movement of food from the mouth to the stomach, and involves the contraction of smooth muscle tissue in the esophagus. In the esophagus, this process is controlled by the medulla oblongata and involves the sequential contraction of the superior, middle, and inferior pharyngeal constrictor muscles. The movement of food in the food pipe is also known as swallowing, which involves the coordination of skeletal and smooth muscles to propel food into the stomach. This process is crucial for the proper digestion and absorption of nutrients.
The movement of food in the food pipe is called peristalsis, which is a radially symmetrical contraction and relaxation of muscles that propagates in a wave down a tube, in an anterograde direction. This process involves the contraction of smooth muscle tissue in the esophagus, which propels the food bolus down the esophagus into the stomach. Peristalsis is controlled by the medulla oblongata and is a complex process involving both skeletal and smooth muscles. In the esophagus, peristalsis occurs in three types: primary, secondary, and tertiary, with each type having a different function and characteristics. Overall, peristalsis plays a crucial role in the digestion and movement of food through the digestive system.

First ans:  Hans Christian Gram developed the Gram staining method to distinguish bacteria into two large groups: Gram-positive and Gram-negative. This method is a useful tool in the identification of bacterial species. Additionally, Robert Koch used a new staining method to reveal the causal agent of tuberculosis, Mycobacterium tuberculosis. Robert Koch's discovery was a significant breakthrough in understanding the disease. First ans:  The first five books of Moses, also known as the Pentateuch, are attributed to Moses in the Hebrew Bible and Jewish tradition. However, modern scholarly consensus rejects Mosaic authorship, affirming that the Pentateuch has multiple authors and was written over centuries. The precise process by which the Pentateuch was composed, the number of authors involved, and the date of each author remain hotly contested. The documentary hypothesis, which posits four independent sources (J, E, P, and D) compiled together by a redactor, has been influential in understanding the composition of the Pentateuch. The final form of the Pentateuch is widely seen as a product of the Persian period, with Ezra playing a pivotal role in its promulgation.
The first five books of Moses, also known as the Torah, are attributed to Moses, with the exception of the last eight verses of Deuteronomy, which are believed to have been written by Joshua. According to Jewish tradition, the Torah was recompiled by Ezra during the Second Temple period, but the modern scholarly consensus rejects Mosaic authorship, suggesting that the Torah has multiple authors and was written over centuries. The documentary hypothesis posits that the first five books were compiled from four independent sources: J, the Jahwist source, E, the Elohist source, P, the Priestly source, and D, the Deuteronomist source. The final formation of the Pentateuch is widely seen as a product of the Persian period, with the Persian authorities requiring the Jews of Jerusalem to present a single body of law as the price of local autonomy.

First ans:  The title "A Raisin in the Sun" was inspired by the poem "Harlem" (also known as "A Dream Deferred") by Langston Hughes. The poem's themes of deferred dreams and the struggles of African Americans resonated with the playwright Lorraine Hansberry, who used the title to capture the essence of her play about a black family's experiences in south Chicago. The title was also influenced by the idea of a raisin, which is a dried-up fruit, symbolizing the dried-up dreams of the characters. Additionally, the title may have been inspired by the idea of a raisin being a small, insignificant thing, but one that still holds a deep significance and value. The title ultimately reflects the themes of hope, struggle, and the pursuit of a better life that are central to the play.
The title "A Raisin in the Sun" was inspired by the poem "A Dream Deferred" by Langston Hughes. The poem explores the idea of a dream that is put on hold, much like a raisin that has been left to dry and wither away. This concept is central to the play, which explores the Younger family's struggles and aspirations as they navigate the challenges of life on the South Side of Chicago. The title captures the sense of longing and yearning that pervades the play, as the characters search for a better life and a sense of belonging.

First ans:  A player can be substituted in soccer during a stoppage in play, with the permission of the referee, and only when the player to be substituted has left the field of play. The substitute must enter the field at the half-way line. This is governed by Law 3 of the Laws of the Game in the (3) Substitution Procedure section. Failure to comply with these provisions may result in a caution (yellow card) being issued by the referee. A player who has been substituted may take no further part in the match.
A player can be substituted in soccer during a stoppage in play and with the permission of the referee. The player to be substituted must have left the field of play before the substitute can enter, and the substitute can only enter the field at the half-way line. This is governed under Law 3 of the Laws of the Game in the (3) Substitution Procedure section. If a player is sent off, they cannot be substituted, and the team must make do with the remaining players. In some competitions, a fourth substitute is allowed in extra time.

The exact identity of the author of Le Morte d'Arthur has been the subject of speculation, but the most widely accepted theory is that the author was Sir Thomas Malory, born in 1416, who was a knight and a prisoner. Malory's actual title for the work was "The Whole Book of King Arthur and His Noble Knights of the Round Table," but it was later changed to Le Morte d'Arthur.
Sir Thomas Malory is credited with writing the romance Le Morte d'Arthur, which tells the story of King Arthur's life and death. However, Malory did not invent the stories in this collection; he translated and compiled them from existing French and English sources, including the Old French Vulgate romances and Middle English sources such as the Alliterative Morte Arthure and the Stanzaic Morte Arthur. Malory's work is considered a reworking of existing tales about King Arthur and his knights, rather than an original creation. The exact identity of the author of Le Morte d'Arthur has been the subject of speculation, but most scholars accept that the author was Sir Thomas Malory, born in 1416.

First ans:  A set of kettledrums in an orchestra is commonly referred to as a console, and it typically consists of four drums: roughly 32 inches, 29 inches, 26 inches, and 23 inches in diameter. This standard set is capable of producing a range of pitches, from D2 to A3. In some cases, a set of two or three timpani, known as the "orchestral three," is used, which consists of 29-inch, 26-inch, and 23-inch drums. This smaller set has a more limited range, extending only down to F2. A console or a set of timpani is an essential component of an orchestra, providing a unique sound and adding depth to the overall musical composition.
A standard set of timpani in an orchestra is typically called a console, and it consists of four drums. A more common term for a set of three timpani, often used in schools and youth orchestras, is the "orchestral three". This set includes 29-inch, 26-inch, and 23-inch drums, and its range extends down to F2. The orchestral three is often used in situations where a full set of timpani is not available. In professional orchestras, the term "console" is more commonly used to refer to a standard set of four timpani.

First ans:  The purpose of a traffic circle, also known as a roundabout, is to provide a safe and efficient way for vehicles to flow in one direction around a central island, while prioritizing traffic already within the junction. In modern roundabouts, entering drivers give way to traffic within the roundabout without the need for traffic signals, reducing the likelihood and severity of collisions. This type of circular intersection or junction is designed to increase safety and reduce traffic congestion. The central island in a roundabout serves as a visual barrier, alerting approaching drivers to the presence of the roundabout and encouraging drivers to focus on the traffic in the path of the circle. Overall, the purpose of a traffic circle is to manage traffic flow and provide a safer and more efficient way for vehicles to navigate intersections.
A traffic circle is a type of circular intersection or junction where road traffic is permitted to flow in one direction around a central island, and priority is typically given to traffic already in the junction. The purpose of a traffic circle is to improve traffic flow and reduce congestion by allowing vehicles to circulate around the central island in one direction. This type of junction is designed to slow down traffic and minimize the likelihood and severity of collisions. Traffic circles are often used to reduce driver confusion associated with perpendicular junctions and reduce queuing associated with traffic lights. They also allow U-turns within the normal flow of traffic, which is not possible at other types of junctions.

This discovery is generally considered the beginning of the modern era of tuberculosis treatment. Later, in 1952, the first oral mycobactericidal drug, isoniazid, was developed, revolutionizing tuberculosis treatment. The advent of rifampin in the 1970s further improved recovery times and reduced the number of tuberculosis cases.

First ans:  The Bill of Rights applies to the states through the Due Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment, which incorporates most provisions of the Bill of Rights, including the Fourth Amendment right to unreasonable search and seizure, the Fifth Amendment right to a fair trial, and the Sixth Amendment right to a speedy and public trial. This incorporation is based on the Supreme Court's interpretation of the Fourteenth Amendment, which was first established in the case of Gitlow v. New York (1925) and has been gradually expanded to include most of the significant provisions of the Bill of Rights. The incorporation process has been incremental, with the Court applying new procedural constitutional rights retroactively against the states in criminal cases with limited exceptions. The Fourteenth Amendment's Due Process Clause has been held to apply to the states through the Privileges or Immunities Clause, which is an alternative basis for incorporation.
The Bill of Rights applies to the states through the Fourteenth Amendment, which includes the Due Process Clause and the Privileges or Immunities Clause. This incorporation was established in the landmark case of McDonald v. Chicago (2010), where the Supreme Court held that the Second Amendment right to keep and bear arms is incorporated through the Due Process Clause. However, some justices have suggested that the Privileges or Immunities Clause might be a more appropriate textual basis for incorporation. In the case of Timbs v. Indiana (2019), the Supreme Court ruled that the Eighth Amendment's Excessive Fines Clause is incorporated through the Due Process Clause, but Justice Thomas noted that he would reach the same incorporation through the Privileges or Immunities Clause. Overall, the incorporation of the Bill of Rights against the states is a complex and evolving doctrine.

First ans:  The National Minimum Drinking Age Act of 1984 was passed by the United States Congress on July 17, 1984, and it was signed into law by President Ronald Reagan on July 17, 1984. This act required all states to enforce a minimum legal drinking age of 21 or else risk losing 10% of all federal highway construction funds. As a result, all 50 states, two permanently inhabited territories, and D.C. were in compliance with the law by 1995. However, some states, such as Alabama, Indiana, Kansas, Michigan, North Carolina, Pennsylvania, and Vermont, and the District of Columbia, extended the law into an outright ban on drinking for those under 21.
Context information is below.    ---------------------    Document: National Minimum Drinking Age ActTitle: N/ADescription: N/AFields:Long title: An Act to encourage a uniform minimum drinking age of 21; to combat drugged driving, improve law enforcement and provide incentives to the states to reduce drunk driving.Acronyms(colloquial): NMDAANicknames: National Minimum Drinking Age act of 1984Enacted by: the98th United States CongressEffective: July 17, 198440 years agoPublic law: 98-363Statutes at Large: 98Stat.435aka98 Stat. 437Titles amended: 23 U.S.C.: HighwaysU.S.C.sections created: 23 U.S.C.ch. 1§ 158Singles Chronology:External Links:H.R. 4616: https://www.congress.gov/bill/98th-congress/house-bill/461681-16: https://www.govtrack.us/congress/votes/98-1984/s529S.Amdt. 3334: https://www.congress.gov/amendment/98th-congress/senate-amendment/3334The National Minimum Drinking Age Act of 1984 (23 U.S.C. § 158) was passed by the United States Congress on July 17, 1984.[1][2][3] The act was a controversial bill that punished every state that allowed persons below 21 years to purchase and publicly possess alcoholic beverages by reducing its annual federal highway apportionment by 10 percent. The law was later amended, lowering the penalty to 8 percent from fiscal year 2012 and beyond.[4]Despite its name, this act did not outlaw the consumption of alcoholic beverages by those under 21 years of age, just its purchase. However, Alabama, Indiana, Kansas, Michigan, North Carolina, Pennsylvania, and Vermont, and the District of Columbia extended the law into an outright ban. The minimum purchase and drinking ages is a state law, and most states still permit "underage" consumption of alcohol in some circumstances. In some states, no restriction on private consumption is made, while in other states, consumption is only allowed in specific locations, in the presence of consenting and supervising family members, as in the states of Colorado, Maryland, Montana, New York, Texas, West Virginia, Washington, Wisconsin, and Wyoming. The act also does not seek to criminalize alcohol consumption during religious occasions (e.g. communion wines, Kiddush).The act was expressly upheld as constitutional in 1987 by the United States Supreme Court in South Dakota v. Dole.Document: National Minimum Drinking Age Act/HistoryLegislation concerning the legal minimum drinking age in the United States can be traced back to the days of Prohibition. In 1920, the 18th amendment to the U.S. Constitution declared it illegal to manufacture, transport, or sell intoxicating liquors.[5] This was repealed with the passing of the 21st Amendment in 1933, which was followed by the adoption of minimum legal drinking age policies in all states, with most states electing a minimum legal drinking age (MLDA) of 21.[5] Between 1970 and 1975, 29 states lowered the MLDA from 21 to 18, 19, or 20. This was primarily due to the passing of the 26th Amendment, which lowered the required voting age from 21 to 18.[5]During the 1960s, both Congress and the state legislatures came under increasing pressure to lower the minimum voting age from 21 to 18. This was in large part due to the Vietnam War, in which many young men who were ineligible to vote (or legally drink) were conscripted to fight in the war, thus lacking any means to influence the people sending them off to risk their lives. "Old enough to fight, old enough to vote," was a common slogan used by proponents of lowering the voting age. The slogan traced its roots to World War II, when President Franklin D. Roosevelt lowered the military draft age to 18. With the lowering of the voting age to 18, the minimum legal drinking age (MLDA) was similarly lowered under the notion that by being able to vote (and for males, be subject to being involuntarily drafted into the enlisted ranks of the military), one should also be able to legally consume alcoholic beverages.However, these changes were soon followed by studies showing an increase in motor vehicle fatalities attributable to the decreased MLDA. In response to these findings, many states raised the minimum legal drinking age to 19 (and sometimes to 20 or 21).[5] In 1984, the National Minimum Legal Drinking Act, written by Senator Frank Lautenberg (D-NJ) and influenced by Mothers Against Drunk Driving (MADD), required all states to enforce a minimum legal drinking age of 21 or else risk losing 10% of all federal highway construction funds.As the MLDA was still left to the discretion of the state, the act did not violate the 21st amendment which reserved the right to regulate alcohol for all responsibilities not specifically appointed to the federal government to the states.[5] However, as the act controlled the distribution of anywhere from $8 million to $99 million, depending on the size of the state, the act gave a strong incentive for states to change the drinking age to 21.[5] By 1995, all 50 states, two permanently inhabited territories, and D.C. were in compliance, but Puerto Rico and the Virgin Islands (and Guam until 2010) remained at 18 despite them losing 10% of federal highway funding.Professor of law Tim Jost noted that the Roberts Court ruling in NFIB v. Sebelius, though upholding South Dakota v. Dole, had serious implications for future laws that incentivize state action.[6]Constitutional lawyer Adam Winkler disagrees saying[7]The Conservative Party of New York opposed the passage of the law in 1984.  In 2001, according to the same article, New York State Assembly member Félix Ortiz introduced a bill that would lower the drinking age back to 18. He cited unfairness and difficulty with enforcement as his motivations.[8]In 1998, the National Youth Rights Association was founded, in part, to seek to lower the drinking age back to 18. In 2004, the president of Vermont's Middlebury College, John McCardell, Jr. wrote in The New York Times that "the 21-year-old drinking age is bad social policy and terrible law" that has made the college drinking problem far worse.[9] Groups that oppose the 21 minimum include Choose Responsibility, the Amethyst Initiative, and Students for Sensible Drug Policy.Mothers Against Drunk Driving supports the 21 law and has been the main opponent to lowering it back to 18. Several public and private universities in the country have also been a major supporter of the 21 law, plus various health organizations such as the American Medical Association.Document: National Minimum Drinking Age Act## Application on college campuses ##College campuses across the nation continue to struggle with issues of underage drinking, despite the nationwide MLDA of 21. The National Institute on Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism (NIAAA) took special interest in this issue, and compiled a list of recommendations for colleges to implement in order to combat underage drinking on campus. However, few schools have actually implemented these recommendations, and according to a recent study, most of the intervention programs currently in place on college campuses have proven ineffective. Underage drinking is nearly impossible to prevent on college campuses because access to alcoholic beverages is extremely easy.[15]Though it is not the only factor that contributes to student drinking, liquor's contraband status seems to imbue it with mystique.  As a result, use and abuse of liquor is seen as sophisticated and is expected.[16]Of the colleges surveyed, 98% offered alcohol education programs to their students. Only 50% of surveyed colleges offered intervention programs, 33% coordinated efforts with the surrounding community to monitor illegal alcohol sales, 15% confirmed that surrounding establishments offered responsible beverage service training, and 7% restricted the number of alcohol outlets within the community. Special services for "problem drinkers" were available at 67% of the surveyed schools; 22% of the schools referred problem drinkers to off-campus resources, and 11% offered no intervention program whatsoever. 34% of the surveyed schools were located in communities which actively instituted compliance checks, but 60% of these checks occurred without university involvement. One fifth of surveyed schools were all together unaware of the NIAAA's recommendations.[15]Many factors may explain colleges' failure to implement the NIAAA's recommendations to control underage drinking on campus: a lack of university funding, a lack of time, a perceived lack of authority or jurisdiction within the community, or even a lack of interest on the part of the university, many universities even see the program as a waste of resources. Whatever the reasons may be, a multitude of options are available should colleges choose to institute programs to decrease instances of underage drinking on campus. These options include, but are not limited to, alcohol education programs, social norms campaigns, substance-free housing, individual interventions, parental notification policies, disciplinary procedures for alcohol-related violations, and amnesty policies to protect the health and safety of students.[15]## Effects ##Several studies, including a 2011 review, provided evidence against the idea that raising the drinking age to 21 actually saved lives in the long run.[17][18][19][20] For example, Miron and Tetelbaum (2009) found that when the federally coerced and non-coerced states were separated out, any lifesaving effect is no longer statistically or practically significant in the coerced states, and even in the voluntary-adopting states the effect does not seem to last beyond the first year or two.  They also find that the 21 drinking age appears to have only a minor impact on teen drinking.[21] There is also some evidence that traffic deaths were merely shifted from the 18-20 age group to the 21-24 age group rather than averted.[22][23][20] Additionally, Canada, Australia, the UK, and several other nations saw similar or faster declines in traffic fatalities than the USA did since the early 1980s despite not raising their drinking ages to 21.[24]  In contrast, the Institute of Medicine reviewed a large number of studies on the minimum legal drinking age, including peer-reviewed academic reviews,[25][26][27] and largely viewed the policy as a success[28]—so much so that they argued for similar restrictions on tobacco. For example, they quote a study by Kypri and colleagues stating that that "No traffic safety policy, with the possible exception of motorcycle safety helmet laws, has more evidence for its effectiveness than do the minimum legal drinking age laws."[29]## See also #### References #### External links ##* Carpenter, Christopher; Dobkin, Carlos (2011). "The Minimum Legal Drinking Age and Public Health". Journal of Economic Perspectives. 25 (2): 133–56. doi:10.1257/jep.25.2.133. PMC 3182479. PMID 21595328.* MLDA-21 - dates enacted by state –  U.S. National Highway Traffic Safety Administration* State Profiles of Underage Drinking Laws –  National Institute on Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism Alcohol Policy Information System (APIS)* Stahl, Lesley (February 22, 2009). "Drinking Age Debate". 60 Minutes. CBS News.* History of the Drinking Age in Washington, D.C.  – Ghosts of DC blog# Alcohol laws of Wisconsin #The alcohol laws of Wisconsin consist of both statewide statutes and local ordinances governing the sale of alcohol.## History ##When Wisconsin became a state, settlers from the eastern United States (known as Yankees) took issue with the consumption of alcohol by German immigrants on Sunday, as well as the prevalence of alcoholism. The Wisconsin legislature passed a law in 1849 that made liquor sellers liable for the costs incurred by local governments in supporting alcoholics. Ten years later, the state prohibited liquor sales on Sundays.[1]In 1872, alcohol regulation reached new heights in the state with the passage of the Graham Law. This legislation prohibited drunkenness and the sale of alcohol to minors, and required all liquor sellers to post a $2,000 bond (more than $30,000 in 2007 U.S. dollars).[2] German-Americans fought the new law in the courts and at the ballot box. Although they lost challenges in the courts, they were able to elect a legislature friendlier to alcohol consumption. In 1874, the new lawmakers passed less restrictive laws that lowered the bond to $500, allowed Sunday liquor sales, and created certain safe havens for liquor sellers to escape liability for alcoholics.[1]Alcohol consumption was banned in Wisconsin during Prohibition (1920-1933).  But even before Prohibition ended, Wisconsin created work-arounds. In 1926, voters approved a referendum allowing the manufacture of beer, if not its consumption. The state repealed its law enforcing Prohibition in 1929.[3] Wisconsin Senator John J. Blaine sponsored the Act that later became the Twenty-first Amendment to the United States Constitution, ending Prohibition.[4] The state was the second to ratify the amendment on April 25, 1933.[5]## Retail sale of alcohol ##State law prohibits retail sale of liquor and wine between 9:00 p.m. and 6:00 a.m., and beer between midnight and 6:00 a.m.[6] State law allows local municipalities to further restrict retail sales of alcohol, or ban the issuance of retail liquor licenses altogether.[7] Local ordinances often prohibit retail beer sale after 9:00 p.m.At least two municipalities in Wisconsin prohibited the retail sale of alcohol until recently: the city of Sparta,[8][9] and the village of Ephraim.[10][11] In the April 1, 2014 Wisconsin spring election, voters in Sparta narrowly passed a referendum to allow the sale of beer and wine in groceries and convenience stores. The ban on the sale of liquor within the city remains in effect.[12] In the April 5, 2016, voters in Ephraim passed a referendum to allow the sale of beer and wine in restaurants and businesses.[13]## Beverage sale of alcohol ##State law requires that bars be closed between 2:00 a.m. and 6:00 a.m. Monday through Friday and between 2:30 a.m. and 6:00 a.m. on Saturday and Sunday. Exceptions are made on New Year's Eve, when no closing is required, and for changes in Daylight Saving Time. State law does not permit municipalities to further restrict when bars must be closed.[6] Municipalities may elect, however, to prohibit the issuance of liquor licenses, making the municipality effectively dry.[7]## Drinking age ##The drinking age in Wisconsin is 21. Those under the legal drinking age may be served, possess, or consume alcohol if they are with a parent, legal guardian, or spouse who is of legal drinking age.[14][15]Those age 18 to 20 may also possess (but not consume) alcohol as part of their employment.[6] In the early 70s the sale of alcohol was reduced to the age of 18.The 1983 Wisconsin Act 74, effective July 1, 1984, created a drinking age of 19. Meeting in special session at the call of the governor, the legislature enacted 1985 Wisconsin Act 337, which raised the drinking age to 21 and brought the state into compliance with the NMDA (National Minimum Drinking Age) on September 1, 1986.[16]The NMDA law was amended to permit an exception for those persons who were between ages 18 and 21 on the effective date of the law. Wisconsin 19- and 20-year-olds were “grandfathered in” by this exception after enactment of Act 337. In effect, the state did not have a uniform age of 21 until September 1, 1988.[16]## See also ##* Dry county* List of dry communities by U.S. state#Wisconsin* Tavern League of Wisconsin## References ##Document: National Minimum Drinking Age Act/HistoryA key cluster of philosophical opposition to the minimum lies in the natural human need for education and experience; young adults do not receive the opportunity to educate themselves and drink responsibly before the age of 21.  A related line of thought emphasizes the importance of individual rights and freedoms.[5]  Another cluster comes from pragmatism, emphasizing the reality that young people are unlikely to stop drinking, and point to statistics on underage drinking as a reason to institute a lower drinking age, which would provide the opportunity to help "young people learn to make healthy and responsible choices".[5] Social environmental theories are also cited; making alcohol a forbidden fruit may encourage more dangerous drinking than would occur if the drinking age were lowered.[10][11]  With a lower drinking age, young people would have access to "publicly moderated drinking environments", rather than "model their behavior after the excessive consumption typical of private student parties",[12] though the perception of excessive drinking on college campuses is often overstated.[12]When brewing magnate Pete Coors raised the drinking age as a campaign issue during the 2004 U.S. Senate race in Colorado, Republican leaders praised his stand on states' rights but distanced themselves from apparent self-interest.[13][14]Document: Legal status of tattooing in the United Laws § 9-34Document: Right-to-work law## U.S. states with right-to-work laws ##The following 27 states have right-to-work laws:[40][41]* Alabama (adopted 1953, Constitution 2016)* Arizona (Constitution, adopted 1946)[42]* Arkansas (Constitution, adopted 1947)* Florida (Constitution, adopted 1944, revised 1968)[43]* Georgia (adopted 1947)* Idaho (adopted 1985)* Indiana[44] (adopted 2012)* Iowa (adopted 1947)* Kansas (Constitution, adopted 1958)* Kentucky (adopted 2017)* Louisiana (adopted 1976)* Michigan (adopted 2012)[45]* Mississippi (Constitution, adopted 1954)* Nebraska (Constitution and statute, adopted 1946)* Nevada (adopted 1951)* North Carolina (adopted 1947)* North Dakota (adopted 1947)* Oklahoma (Constitution, adopted 2001)* South Carolina[46] (adopted 1954)* South Dakota (adopted 1946)* Tennessee (adopted 1947)* Texas[47] (adopted 1947, revised 1993)* Utah (adopted 1955)* Virginia (adopted 1947)* West Virginia (adopted 2016)[48]* Wisconsin (adopted 2015)* Wyoming (adopted 1963)In addition, the territory of Guam also has right-to-work laws, and employees of the US federal government have the right to choose whether or not to join their respective unions.[49]### Local or repealed laws ###Some states had right-to-work laws in the past, but repealed them or had them declared invalid. There are also some counties and municipalities located in states without right-to-work laws that have passed local laws to ban union security agreements.#### Delaware ####Seaford passed a right-to-work ordinance in 2018.[50]#### Illinois ####Lincolnshire passed a local right-to-work ordinance, but it was struck down by the Seventh Circuit Court of Appeals.[51] An appeal to the Supreme Court resulted in the case being vacated as being moot because in the intervening period Illinois had passed the Illinois Collective Bargaining Freedom Act to invalidate such local ordinances.[52][53]#### Indiana ####Before its passage in 2012, the Republican-controlled Indiana General Assembly passed a right-to-work bill in 1957, which led to the Democratic takeover of Indiana's Governor's Mansion and General Assembly in the coming elections, and eventually, the new Democrat-controlled legislature repealing the right-to-work law in 1965.[54] Right-to-work was subsequently reenacted in 2012.[44]#### Kentucky ####On November 18, 2016, the Sixth Circuit Court of Appeals upheld the right of local governments to enact local right-to-work laws in Kentucky. Kentucky had 12 local ordinances. A statewide law was subsequently enacted in 2017.[55]#### Missouri ####The legislature passed a right-to-work bill in 2017, but the law was defeated in a 2018 referendum before it could take effect.[56][57][58][59]#### New Hampshire ####New Hampshire adopted a right-to-work bill in 1947, but it was repealed in 1949 by the state legislature and governor.[60]#### New Mexico ####New Mexico law was previously silent on local right-to-work laws, and Chaves, Eddy, Lea, Lincoln, McKinley, Otero, Roosevelt, Sandoval, San Juan, and Sierra counties, in addition to Ruidoso village adopted such laws.[61][62][63][64][65] But in 2019 Governor Grisham signed legislation that prohibits local right-to-work laws and further states that union membership and the payment of union dues may be required as a condition of employment in workplaces subject to a collective bargaining agreement.[66]#### West Virginia ####West Virginia passed a right-to-work law in 2016. In 2019 a circuit court struck down the law, but the state Supreme Court stayed that ruling pending appeal, keeping the law in effect.[67]## See also ##* At-will employment* Closed shop and open shop* Labor unions in the United States* United States labor law## Further reading ##* Garcia, R. J. (2019). "Right-to-Work Laws: Ideology and Impact." He was appointed Prime Minister by King Victor Emmanuel III on October 31, 1922, and formed a new government with the support of the fascist movement. Mussolini's appointment marked the beginning of a new era in Italian politics, with the fascist party dominating the government and implementing policies that would shape the country for years to come. Mussolini's rise to power was a result of his charismatic leadership and the promise of a new and modern Italy, which resonated with many Italians who were disillusioned with the old parliamentary system. Mussolini's government would go on to implement policies that would transform Italy into a fascist state, with Mussolini as the dictator.

Mussolini's rise to power was a result of a combination of his charisma, the popularity of his party, and the support of the King.

First ans:  The first Tesla electric car, the Tesla Roadster, was developed from 2004 through 2007, with initial studies done in two "test mule" vehicles based on Lotus Elises equipped with all-electric drive systems. The first production car, Tesla Roadster P1, was built prior to the start of regular series production, which began on March 17, 2008. The first delivery of the Tesla Roadster was made in February 2008 to Elon Musk, the company's co-founder, chairman, and product architect. Tesla delivered its first 500 Roadsters through June 2009, and the company made its first profit in July 2009. The Roadster was the first highway-legal, mass-produced all-electric car to use lithium-ion battery cells and travel more than 320 kilometers per charge.

First ans:  The Pure Food and Drug Act was passed by the 59th United States Congress, which was led by President Theodore Roosevelt. The law was a key piece of Progressive Era legislation, and its enforcement was assigned to the Bureau of Chemistry in the U.S. Department of Agriculture, which was later renamed the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA). The law was largely replaced by the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act of 1938. The 59th Congress was a bipartisan body, with both Republicans and Democrats contributing to the law's passage. The law was also influenced by public interest guardians such as Upton Sinclair and Samuel Hopkins Adams, who exposed the problems with foods and drugs in the U.S.
 82%|████████▏ | 164/200 [2:11:04<29:09, 48.58s/it] 82%|████████▎ | 165/200 [2:11:45<26:54, 46.13s/it] 83%|████████▎ | 166/200 [2:11:59<20:42, 36.55s/it]The Pure Food and Drug Act was passed by the 59th United States Congress and signed into law by President Theodore Roosevelt on the same day as the Federal Meat Inspection Act. The law was a key piece of Progressive Era legislation, and its enforcement was assigned to the Bureau of Chemistry in the U.S. Department of Agriculture, which was later renamed the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA). The Act was largely replaced by the more comprehensive Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act of 1938. The law was a result of public education and exposés from public interest guardians such as Upton Sinclair, Samuel Hopkins Adams, and Harvey W. Wiley. The law was a significant consumer protection law that banned foreign and interstate traffic in adulterated or mislabeled food and drug products.

First ans:  The ophthalmic nerve exits the skull through the superior orbital fissure and the supraorbital notch to provide sensory innervation for the skin of the forehead and scalp. It also exits the skull through the anterior ethmoidal foramen, where it enters the nasal cavity and provides innervation for much of the anterior nasal mucosa. The nasociliary branch of the ophthalmic nerve gives off several sensory branches to the orbit before continuing out through the anterior ethmoidal foramen. Additionally, the lacrimal nerve passes through the orbit superiorly to innervate the lacrimal gland. The ophthalmic nerve also communicates with the oculomotor, trochlear, and abducent nerves through the cavernous plexus of the sympathetic.
The ophthalmic nerve exits the skull through the superior orbital fissure and the supraorbital notch. This occurs after it reenters the frontal bone briefly before exiting. The nerve then continues above the orbit to provide sensory innervation for the skin of the forehead and scalp. It also provides sensory innervation for the skin of the eyelids, eyebrow, and nose.

First ans:  The muscle fibers under the control of a single motor axon are referred to as a motor unit. A motor unit is made up of a motor neuron and the skeletal muscle fibers innervated by that motor neuron's axonal terminals.In vertebrates, the force of a muscle contraction is controlled by the number of activated motor units. The motor units within a muscle are considered a motor pool, and groups of motor units often work together to coordinate the contractions of a single muscle.Each motor unit is composed of muscle fibers of the same fiber type, and when a motor unit is activated, all of its fibers contract. The number of muscle fibers within each unit can vary, with muscles that act on larger body masses having motor units with more muscle fibers.The motor units are organized and controlled by the central nervous system, which is responsible for the orderly recruitment of motor neurons, beginning with the smallest motor units. The size principle indicates that motor units are recruited from smallest to largest based on the size of the load.The motor units can be categorized based on their physiological, biochemical, and histochemical properties, with different types of muscle fibers having distinct characteristics, such as contraction speed, rate of rise of force, and time to peak of a twitch contraction.

A motor unit consists of a motor neuron and the skeletal muscle fibers innervated by that motor neuron's axonal terminals.In vertebrates, the force of a muscle contraction is controlled by the number of activated motor units, with each motor unit containing all muscle fibers of the same fiber type. This means that all muscle fibers within a motor unit contract simultaneously when activated.The number of muscle fibers within each unit can vary, with muscles that act on the largest body masses having motor units with more muscle fibers, while smaller muscles contain fewer muscle fibers in each motor unit.The motor unit is a fundamental unit of muscle control, and its activation is essential for voluntary movements. The orderly recruitment of motor neurons, starting with the smallest motor units, is responsible for the precise control of muscle contractions.Each motor unit is responsible for generating a specific force output, with larger motor units generating higher forces than smaller ones.

First ans:  The phrase "manifest destiny" was coined by John L. O'Sullivan, a journalist and advocate for Jacksonian democracy, in 1845. O'Sullivan's original conception of manifest destiny was that the expansion of the United States would happen without the direction of the U.S. government or the involvement of the military, and that Americans would set up new democratic governments in new regions and then seek admission to the United States. However, the term became popularized and interpreted in different ways, including as a call to territorial expansion and conquest, which was not O'Sullivan's original intention. Manifest destiny was used to justify the annexation of Texas, the war with Mexico, and the division of Oregon with Great Britain, among other events. Ultimately, the meaning of manifest destiny was a contested concept, with some seeing it as a moral mission to spread democracy and others viewing it as a justification for aggressive expansionism.
The phrase "manifest destiny" was coined by John L. O'Sullivan, an influential advocate for Jacksonian democracy, in his article "Annexation" in the Democratic Review in 1845. O'Sullivan used the phrase to describe the United States' divine mission to expand its territory and spread its democratic values across North America. However, the concept of manifest destiny was not a specific policy, but rather a general idea that was interpreted and applied in various ways by different individuals and groups. It was often used to justify the expansion of the United States into new territories, including Texas, Oregon, and Mexico. Despite its popularity, manifest destiny was a contested concept, with many prominent Americans, including Abraham Lincoln and Ulysses S. Grant, rejecting it.

First ans:  The Great Fire of London swept through the central parts of the English city from Sunday, 2 September to Thursday, 6 September 1666. The fire started at the bakery of Thomas Farriner on Pudding Lane shortly after midnight on Sunday, 2 September. It spread rapidly west across the City of London, destroying 13,200 houses, 87 parish churches, St Paul's Cathedral, and most of the buildings of the City authorities. The fire continued to burn for several days, with the wind dropping on Tuesday evening and the firebreaks created by the garrison finally beginning to take effect on Wednesday 5 September. The fire was eventually brought under control, but not before it had destroyed a significant portion of the City of London.
The Great Fire of London swept through the central parts of the English city from Sunday, 2 September to Thursday, 6 September 1666. The fire started at the bakery of Thomas Farriner on Pudding Lane shortly after midnight on Sunday, 2 September, and spread rapidly west across the City of London. The fire was finally brought under control on Wednesday, 5 September, but it took some time until the last traces were put out. Coal was still burning in cellars two months later, and Pepys recorded that he saw smoke remaining, coming out of some cellars, from the late great fire, now above six months since.

First ans:  Peter Anthony Allen and Gwynne Owen Evans were the last people to be hanged in England, on 13 August 1964, for the murder of John Alan West. They were hanged in different prisons, Allen at Walton Prison in Liverpool and Evans at Strangeways Prison in Manchester. Prior to their executions, the last woman to be hanged in England was Ruth Ellis, on 13 July 1955, for the murder of her lover David Blakely. Ellis was hanged at Holloway Prison in London.
Peter Anthony Allen and Gwynne Owen Evans were the last two people hanged in England on 13 August 1964. They were executed for the murder of John Alan West. Prior to their execution, the last woman to be hanged in England was Ruth Ellis on 13 July 1955. The death penalty was suspended for murder in 1965 and finally abolished in 1969, but it remained a legally defined punishment for certain offences such as treason until it was completely abolished in 1998.

Bogardus created the Bogardus social distance scale to empirically measure people's willingness to participate in social contacts of varying degrees of closeness with members of diverse social groups. He used this scale to evaluate attitude towards social distance. However, the scale has been criticized for being oversimplified and not accurately measuring people's actual behavior. Additionally, the assumption of equal differences in quantitative social distance between various options complicates any effort at generalization and comparison. The Bogardus social distance scale is only an attempt to measure one's feeling of unwillingness to associate equally with a group.
Emory S. Bogardus created the Bogardus social distance scale to empirically measure people's willingness to participate in social contacts of varying degrees of closeness with members of diverse social groups. He used this scale to evaluate attitude through social distance, considering it as a function of affective distance between the members of two groups. The scale asks people the extent to which they would be accepting of each group, with a score of 1.00 indicating no social distance. However, the scale has been criticized for its oversimplification and assumption of equal differences in quantitative social distance between various options. First ans:  The creation of the senate was based on the Roman Kingdom's tribal communities, which included an aristocratic board of tribal elders. The early Roman gentes aggregated to form a common community, and the patres from the leading clans were selected for the confederated board of elders that would become the Roman senate. The Roman Senate was also modeled after the ancient Roman Kingdom's system of governance, where the Senate was an assembly of the senior and therefore allegedly wiser and more experienced members of the society or ruling class. The U.S. Senate was designed as a more deliberative body than the U.S. House, with members serving six-year terms to give them more authority to ignore mass sentiment in favor of the country's broad interests. The equal suffrage in the Senate was a compromise, a "lesser evil," and not born out of any political theory, but rather a result of a spirit of amity and mutual deference.
The creation of the Roman Senate was based on the idea of an assembly of elders, where the patres from the leading clans were selected to form a confederated board of elders. The concept of the Roman Senate was later adopted in the United States Senate, which was designed as a more deliberative body than the House of Representatives, with a focus on cooler, more systematic, and wiser decision-making. The Roman Senate was also influenced by the British system of governance, which was admired by the Founding Fathers of the United States. The idea of the Roman Senate was revived in the United States Senate, which was established as a bicameral Congress with a compromise between equal representation and population-based representation. The Roman Senate's concept of an assembly of elders was also adopted in the modern concept of a senate, which is a deliberative assembly that provides a chamber of "sober second thought" to consider legislation passed by a lower house.

First ans:  The latitudes that experience the greatest annual change in daylight hours are those near the Arctic and Antarctic Circles, specifically between 66° and 72° north or south latitude. These regions experience extreme variations in daylight hours due to their proximity to the poles and the resulting extreme tilt of the sun's rays. Within this range, the latitudes between 70° and 72° experience the most dramatic changes in daylight hours, with some areas experiencing up to 24 hours of daylight in the summer and complete darkness in the winter. The Arctic Circle at 66°33'50.2" N and the Antarctic Circle at 66°33'50.2" S are the boundaries beyond which the sun remains above or below the horizon for 24 hours during the respective solstices. The variation in daylight hours decreases as one moves towards the equator, with the equator itself experiencing a relatively constant 12 hours of daylight throughout the year.
The latitudes that experience the greatest annual change in daylight hours are those near the Arctic and Antarctic Circles, specifically between 66° and 72° north and south latitude. At these latitudes, the duration of daylight varies significantly between the summer and winter solstices, with some areas experiencing up to 24 hours of daylight in the summer and complete darkness in the winter. The Tropic of Cancer and Tropic of Capricorn, located at 23°26' north and south latitude respectively, also experience significant changes in daylight hours, but to a lesser extent than the higher latitudes. The middle latitudes, between 30° and 60° north and south, experience more moderate changes in daylight hours, with the difference between summer and winter solstices being around 4 hours.
